# PLAYBOOK — how our agent team builds a project together

**Version: 12.2 — 2026-10-05.** Changelog in §16. A correction from the lead becomes a version
bump in the same session — an unwritten rule dies with the session, and the version number — a
point version written `<major>.<n>` — is how every copy detects drift (§15). **This file is self-contained and it is the only
file that travels:** every project copy is **byte-identical** to the canonical one in the
`ai_playbook` repository. The project's instruction files — `AGENTS.md` holding §C, `CLAUDE.md`
and `GEMINI.md` holding the one line `@AGENTS.md` — are generated by the bootstrap and regenerated
at every bump, never carried, never hand-edited; the toolkit is extracted inside each project from
annex §A, which carries every tool's full source. Nothing here is adapted per project; project
facts, hazards, grants and rules live in the current plan (§1).

**Read this first.** Everything here exists so that what we ship works and nobody has to redo
it — not to slow anyone down. Rigour is proportional: heavy where a mistake is expensive and
hard to reverse, light where it is cheap and obvious. Where a plan and this playbook overlap,
both apply; the plan's explicit, dated derogations win, then the instruction file (§C), then this file. When a
rule and the lead's stated direction collide, the direction wins and you name the rule's cost
once. Two standing-law files that disagree are a defect to fix, not a choice to navigate.

**What we measure.** Three numbers, the same in every project, read from the records by the
tools and by the bump's audit — never kept by hand in the plan:
- **Fast** — wall clock per task (`plan.py` stamps each block's start and end) and per complete
  test loop (`verify.py report`).
- **Smooth** — D blocks opened per task done, questions to the lead and BLOCKED turns per task.
- **Cheap** — the money a DONE block costs, its sessions and the D blocks traced to it
  (`rung_record.py`, from the agent's session files where it can read them, §0), beside the lines
  an agent reads before it starts: the instruction file, its start read (§4 Rule 1) and its
  `Read:` line; per gate, the gate's whole read.
A rule that adds reading or a step per task names the saving that pays for it, or it stays out.

---

## §B How to use this playbook

### §B.1 For the people running it — read this once
- **What it is.** One file that sets up a project so a team of AI coding agents builds it with
  you, one task at a time. Drop it into a repository and start an agent: the first session writes
  the plan, and each later session does one task from it.
- **Run it in a coding agent, in its automatic mode.** Use an agent that reads and edits files and
  runs shell commands in the repository, in the mode that edits and runs commands without asking
  each time **and still lets the agent ask you questions** — §B.3 names that mode for each agent.
  The mode is a tool setting, never a grant: git writes, paid runs, runs over 10 minutes and
  anything irreversible are still asked (§4 Rule 4).
- **One fresh session per task.** Start each task in a new agent session, or clear the session's
  context first, then paste the task's launch line (§14.5): « Read
  milestones/m<N>/m<N>_implementation_plan.md and execute M<N>-<id> yourself … ». A session that
  carries an earlier task reads more and mixes the two.
- **Several tasks at the same time.** Only the tasks the plan marks as one parallel set
  (`∥ set-X`, §6) run at once: start one session per task, each with its own launch line. They
  never edit the plan; each writes its handoff atop its own report, and one session pastes them in
  afterwards. Everything else runs one task at a time, top to bottom. To hand a whole batch to one
  session, launch it as an orchestrator (§7, §14.5).
- **Your part.** You pick the model and level each session runs on; each task's heading recommends
  one, from the models and levels you told the bootstrap you run (§0) — in Claude Code a
  heading's ` · switch` sets the model and the level of every request of that task's session for
  you, once you have installed the switch on the machine (§B.3), and the agent shows you what ran;
  delete ` · switch` to run that task on your pick.
  The plan asks you only what it
  cannot settle itself, a few questions at a time, in plain words. A block tagged `LEAD` waits on
  you: `go` (you approve before a push, a paid or long run, a live plan's edit), `walk` (you drive
  the app while an agent takes notes), `answers` (the block closes on your answers). You commit
  and push at each phase's commit gate (§10).
- **Keeping projects current.** A correction you give becomes a new version of this file the same
  day (§15); the bump updates every project's copy and adds the adoption task to each live plan.

### §B.2 Bootstrap a new project
Drop `PLAYBOOK.md` — one file, **unchanged** — into the new repo root, then launch one PLAN
session with:

> Read PLAYBOOK.md. My project is: \<one paragraph\>. Bootstrap it.

The bootstrap agent:

0. **Writes the instruction files from §C, byte for byte** — `AGENTS.md` holds §C; `CLAUDE.md`
   and `GEMINI.md` each hold the one line `@AGENTS.md`, so every agent in §B.3 loads the same rules
   at session start, whichever one the lead runs later. They are never edited by hand and never
   copied between projects; a bump regenerates them (§15). From the repository root (`py -3` in
   place of `python3` on Windows):

   ```bash
   python3 -c "import re,os,sys;t=open('PLAYBOOK.md',encoding='utf-8').read();m=re.search(r'^## \xa7C[^\n]*\n+\x60{3}markdown\n(.*?)\n\x60{3}\n',t,re.M|re.S);W=dict(zip(('AGENTS.md','CLAUDE.md','GEMINI.md'),(m.group(1)+'\n','@AGENTS.md\n','@AGENTS.md\n'))) if m else {};bad=[f for f in W if os.path.exists(f) and not open(f,encoding='utf-8').read()==W[f]];[open(f,'w',encoding='utf-8',newline='\n').write(W[f]) for f in W if f not in bad];print('written:',len(W)-len(bad),'- refused:',len(bad),*bad);ok=bool(W) and not bad;print('=== GO ===' if ok else '=== NO-GO: '+(str(len(bad))+' refused - archive it first (step 1)' if W else 'no section C block')+' ===');sys.exit(0 if ok else 1)"
   ```

   It writes the three files with LF endings and refuses one that already holds other text —
   step 1 moves that file to `archive/`, then the line runs again.
1. **Retires competing playbooks.** Any pre-existing methodology or rules document moves to
   `archive/` with a one-line supersession note in the tracker. TP re-checks this at every plan
   open (§2.1).
2. **Interviews the lead** with the question tool (§12), up to four questions per call, one
   decision each: stack and target platforms · dev shells and machines in play (per-box verdicts,
   §8) · product/UI language · repo state (green field or existing code) · real user data
   (→ OPT-D) · paid client or resale (→ OPT-G) · one product line or several tracks (→ OPT-E /
   OPT-F) · optional modules (§13) · milestone size class (§2.5) · the `Changes by asking:` line
   (§0) · **the ladder** (§0): the models and levels the lead runs in this agent, their usual pair
   and their pair for gates — first the probe (§B.3) and this agent's model pages, read on the
   day, so the question recommends what they found · standing self-run grants (OPT-C) · anything
   the description left open. A question that freezes a clause restates its consequence in one
   plain sentence (§12). Never invent an answer.
3. **Authors** the tracker (`milestones.md` by default — its name goes in the plan's Repo facts)
   and `milestones/m0/m0_implementation_plan.md` from the skeleton (§14.1), sized per §2.5, with
   the M0 goal distilled from the description and the interview, its `Models:` line the ladder,
   every block it writes rated (§0) — an S plan's whole pipeline, which no TB re-rates. Where the
   ladder's agent is Claude Code, writes the switch's hook line (§B.3), puts ` · switch` after
   every rating it writes (§3.2), and names to the lead the install, once per machine, and any
   rung of the ladder the switch's `RUNGS` lacks (§B.3). Creates
   `milestones/m0/{logs,reports,tasks,images}/` now (§1). What the interview settled (commands,
   ports, shells, boxes) goes into the plan's Repo facts, where `M0-TH` reads it to extract the
   toolkit and write its manifests (annex §A, §B.4).
4. **Stops.** No M0 task runs in this session — one fresh session per task (§4 Rule 0). Ends
   with the first task's launch prompt, ready to paste (§14.5), and this line for the lead, once:
   "This plan and its tasks run best in a coding agent with file and shell tools, in its automatic
   mode — one that still lets the agent ask you questions (for this agent: <§B.3's mode>). Start
   each task in a fresh session."

### §B.3 What differs by agent — read once, by the bootstrap agent
The rest of this file uses neutral words: *the instruction file* (the file an agent loads by
itself at session start), *the question tool* (the agent's structured question tool: up to four
questions, options, the recommendation first), *sub-agent*, *agent memory*, *the level* (how hard
the model thinks — the agent's effort, reasoning effort or variant). Where agents differ,
this table says how. Without a question tool, the agent writes its questions in its message as
numbered options, recommendation first, and ends the turn `IN PROGRESS (awaiting: <question>)`;
an automatic mode that answers questions for the lead, or blocks the question tool, is never used.

| Agent | Loads at start | Question tool | Automatic mode that still asks | Sub-agents | Agent memory | Session process | Model and level — the lead sets them · a session reads its own |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Claude Code | `CLAUDE.md` → `@AGENTS.md` | `AskUserQuestion` | `auto` — not `dontAsk`, which blocks the question tool | yes | auto memory, on by default | `claude` | `--model`, `--effort` at launch; `/model`, `/effort`, or the prompt box's picker in VS Code · levels `low` `medium` `high` `xhigh` `max`, calibrated per model (Haiku 4.5 takes none) · reads its level in `$CLAUDE_EFFORT`, its agent in `$CLAUDECODE`; its model only from its own instructions, else ask · applies a heading's rating itself: the switch, below |
| Gemini CLI | `GEMINI.md` → `@AGENTS.md` | `ask_user` (1–4 questions, 2–4 options) | `auto_edit`, or `--yolo` | yes | — | `gemini` (unverified) | `--model`, `/model`, or Auto (a model picked per request) · the level only in settings (`thinkingLevel`: `low` `medium` `high`) · reads its agent in `$GEMINI_CLI`; model and level: ask |
| OpenAI Codex | `AGENTS.md` | plan mode's clarifying questions | "Auto" | yes | memories, off by default | `codex` | `--model`, `/model` (the level too) · `model_reasoning_effort`: `low` to `max`, then `ultra` · reads neither — `/status` shows them to the lead; ask |
| GitHub Copilot | `AGENTS.md` | VS Code `askQuestions`; CLI: plan mode | VS Code "Allow all" — not "Autopilot", which answers for you; CLI `/allow-all` | CLI: yes | Copilot Memory | `copilot` (CLI); inside the editor otherwise | CLI `--model` (`auto`: a model picked per prompt, within a tier), `--effort` `low` to `max`, `/model` · the editor's own control: unverified · reads neither: ask |
| Cursor | `AGENTS.md` (its CLI also reads `CLAUDE.md`) | plan mode's clarifying questions | "Run Everything", or "Auto-review" | yes | — | `agent` (CLI); inside the editor otherwise | CLI `--model`, `/model`, or Auto · the level is the model's thinking variant · reads neither: ask |
| OpenCode | `AGENTS.md` | `question` | its default (every tool runs without asking) | — | — | `opencode` (unverified) | `--model <provider>/<model>`, `/models` · the level `--variant`, by provider · reads neither: ask |
| JetBrains Junie · Google Antigravity | `AGENTS.md` | none found in their docs | — | — | — | Junie: inside the editor · Antigravity: `agy`, its CLI (the process name unverified) | Junie `--model`, `--effort` (`minimal` to `max`), `/model`, `/effort`; Antigravity one picker, the level in the model's label · reads neither: ask |

An agent this table does not list: the bootstrap writes §C to the file that agent loads (still
generated, still identical everywhere) and the next bump adds its row.

**Claude Code's switch** (§0, §3.2) — annex §A.10's `tools/pb/switch.mjs`, a plugin of the
client's function hooks (marked early access: "may change between releases without notice").
Installed, it sets the model and the level of every main-loop request of a task session to the
rung its block's heading rates where ` · switch` follows the rating — the launch turn's first
request and the first after each prompt the lead types included, which no skill and no call of
the agent's reaches (v12.1's skill moved the level and never the model; §16).
- **What it reads:** the session's first typed prompt, its tags stripped — §14.5's `execute
  M<N>-<id>` or the lead's own `do task <id>` — one id, whole or local; the plan, the one `.md`
  path that prompt names, else the file the editor had open; that id's heading, outside code
  fences, exactly one; the rating after the type tag when ` · switch` follows it, mapped through
  the file's `RUNGS` table to a model id and a level. One decision per session: the plan is read
  once, a later prompt never moves it, `/clear` starts a new session.
- **What it leaves alone:** a sub-agent's request; and on any miss — no id, two ids, no plan, no
  single heading, no ` · switch`, a rung `RUNGS` lacks, a read that fails — every request goes on
  untouched, on the lead's pick. Deleting ` · switch` from a heading is the lead's override.
- **Where it held** — read from the session file's per-request `message.model` and `effort`,
  Claude Code 2.1.289, 2026-10-05: a test plugin on the same hook, installed in VS Code at project
  scope, set 7 of 7 requests — the launch, an answered question, a message sent mid-turn, a typed
  follow-up — while the picker showed another model and level; headless with `--plugin-dir`,
  every request of four sessions, a typed follow-up and a sub-agent's request included;
  `switch.mjs` itself, headless, 10 of 10 requests on the rung across the launch line and the
  lead's form (the opened plan + `do task <id>`), and 4 of 4 left on the launch flags with
  ` · switch` deleted. It loaded **nowhere** from a project's settings or its `.claude/skills/`
  (VS Code offered no install), nor in a client process started before the install: `/clear`
  keeps the process (a session after `/clear` in a process 26 minutes older than the install ran
  every request on the picker's model). An install at user scope, loaded in VS Code: a session
  started after it ran 58 of 58 requests on the rung's model and level, the picker on another
  model (Claude Code 2.1.289, 2026-10-05).
- **What a project carries:** `tools/pb/switch.mjs`, extracted with the toolkit (§B.4) and swapped
  at a bump like every annex tool, and the check's hook line below in `.claude/settings.json` —
  no skill, no plugin folder in the tree. **The install is the lead's, once per machine** (an
  install is never an agent's): `rung_record.py now` prints the line when the plugin is `missing`
  or `stale` (§A.9) — `node tools/pb/switch.mjs build` first, into the client's own folder
  (`<CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR or ~/.claude>/pb-switch-src`, never a checkout's), then the client's own
  binary — in VS Code the extension's `resources/native-binary/claude`, never an older `claude` on
  the PATH — `plugin marketplace add "<that folder>"` and `plugin install pb-switch@pb --scope
  user` where none is installed (`missing`), `plugin update pb-switch@pb --scope user` where a copy is
  (`stale`: `install` on an installed id only says so and leaves the old version recorded), then a new
  session (not `/clear`). `RUNGS` is one table per machine: a rung a ladder adds
  is added there (a tool edit, §A.10), then built and installed again; until then that rung's
  blocks run on the lead's pick.
- **Its price, per task session:** the switch itself nothing — no text in the agent's context, no
  tool call, no wait (per request a table lookup; on a session's first request one read of its
  transcript and one of the plan). The check: the hook's line on every typed prompt (≈840 B) and
  one `now` call with its line (≈95 B) — ≈0.9 kB, ≈235 tokens, one tool call and 2 s a typed
  prompt, against v12.1's ≈1.45 kB, ≈360 tokens, three tool calls and 2 s, which never set the
  model. The saving that pays for it: the block runs on its rated model and level from its first
  request, with no picker step and no relaunch (expected, not yet measured).
- **The check** — the lead's ask: the agent shows which model and level ran. One UserPromptSubmit
  hook in `.claude/settings.json` (other keys kept), a command that prints one line for the agent:
  "PLAYBOOK switch: when your block's heading has '· switch' after its rating, run 'sleep 2;
  python3 tools/pb/rung_record.py now' alone in its Bash call on every prompt the lead types (on
  the launch prompt right after the grep of your heading, before plan.py show), so its line shows
  in the chat: the model or the level not the heading's rung, say MISMATCH, naming both, and carry
  on; plugin=missing, give the lead the install line it printed, tagged NOT RUN — for the lead, and
  carry on; the file not there yet, say NOT RUN (no rung_record.py yet), the handoff's model and
  level reading the same, and carry on. Your handoff's model and level quote that line's model= and
  level=, a commit's attribution its model= — never the identity or attribution text in your
  context, never the picker, never your own claim. No '· switch': carry on." It reads nothing and
  decides nothing. The bump's roll-out writes it into the folder the lead's sessions start in — a
  checkout's, or the folder above it where the hook already stands — and where that folder is not
  the root that holds `tools/pb/`, the line names `rung_record.py` by the path from that folder
  (`<checkout>/notebooks/tools/pb/rung_record.py`), its dry run ending the folder's line `· check:
  <path>`; before a project's toolkit swap has extracted the file, the line's clause above says so.
A handoff on a switched block names the rung and the `now` line — the model and the level the
session's file holds for the running request — a model other than the rung's said so. That line's
`model=` is the model the handoff and a commit's attribution quote, on a MISMATCH too: never the
identity or attribution text in the agent's context, never the picker, never the model's own
claim (measured 2026-10-05: an identity line and a `Co-Authored-By:` line on one model while every
request of the session ran on another). `rung_record.py now` (§A.9) reads the session's own record,
the last main-loop request's `message.model` and `effort`, and prints one line, `model=<id>
level=<level> plugin=<installed|missing|unknown>`, alone in its Bash call so the chat shows it; on
`missing` a second line, `install: <command>`, for the machine it runs on. It waits 2 s first: the
session's file can trail the running request by one message (measured 2026-10-05, Claude Code
2.1.289: without the wait it read the previous request's level 2 times of 2, with it the running
request's 3 times of 3). `plugin=installed` names the install, not the running process: installed
with the model a MISMATCH, the session's process predates the install (above) — the lead starts a
new session. Before TH or a TH-adopt extracts the tool, the check reads `NOT RUN (no rung_record.py yet)`.
The Bash environment holds the level (`$CLAUDE_EFFORT`) and no model, and the hook inputs are no
detector: in the measured run SessionStart carried no model and UserPromptSubmit no level.
**Retired at v12.2:** the six `pb-*` skills, the agent's paired `sleep 2` and skill call, and the
lead's typed `/pb-<rung>` — an adoption deletes `.claude/skills/pb-*` and replaces the old hook
line (§14.3).

**The probe** — what a session can read about itself, before the bootstrap asks for the ladder
(§B.2 step 2) and when an adoption or a rating pass re-reads it (§0). From the repository root:

```bash
python3 -c "import os;K=('CLAUDECODE','CLAUDE_EFFORT','GEMINI_CLI','AI_AGENT','AGENT','ANTHROPIC_MODEL','COPILOT_MODEL','GEMINI_MODEL','JUNIE_MODEL','JUNIE_EFFORT');print(*[k+'='+os.environ[k] for k in K if os.environ.get(k)] or ['none set'],sep='\n')"
```

It prints the agent and level variables that are set. A model or level saved in the agent's
settings, or a `*_MODEL` variable the lead exported, is a hint, never the session's own value;
what the probe cannot read, the bootstrap asks.

### §B.4 The toolkit's one line — extraction from annex §A
From the repository root (`py -3` in place of `python3` on Windows):

```bash
python3 -c "import re,sys,os,hashlib;t=open(sys.argv[1],encoding='utf-8').read();d=sys.argv[2];B=re.findall(r'^<\x21-- pb:file (tools/pb/[\w./-]+) md5:([0-9a-f]{12}) -->\n~~~~[\w-]*\n(.*?)^~~~~\n',t,re.M|re.S);bad=[p for p,h,b in B if '..' in p.split('/') or not hashlib.md5(b.encode()).hexdigest().startswith(h)];[(os.makedirs(os.path.dirname(os.path.join(d,p)),exist_ok=True),open(os.path.join(d,p),'w',encoding='utf-8',newline='\n').write(b),os.chmod(os.path.join(d,p),0o755 if b.startswith('#\x21') else 0o644)) for p,h,b in B if p not in bad];print('extracted:',len(B)-len(bad),'- refused:',len(bad),*bad);ok=bool(B) and not bad;print('=== GO ===' if ok else '=== NO-GO: '+(str(len(bad))+' refused' if B else 'no marked block')+' ===');sys.exit(0 if ok else 1)" PLAYBOOK.md <dest>
```

It writes every marked file of annex §A under `<dest>` — `.` in a new project, a temp folder
first in one that already has tools (§14.3 TH-adopt) — with LF endings, `0755` for a file that
starts `#!`; it refuses a block whose md5 differs or whose path climbs, deletes nothing, and ends
in the §4 Rule 4 verdict.

---

## §C The instruction file — generated from this block by the bootstrap (never hand-edited, never carried)

```markdown
# How we work here
<!-- Mirrored from PLAYBOOK.md v12.2 (§C). Identical in every project — never edited here.
     Project facts, hazards and grants live in the current plan's ## Repo facts and ## Rules.
     Precedence: the plan's dated derogations > this block > PLAYBOOK.md. Open PLAYBOOK.md
     only when a line below points there. -->

You're one of a team of agents finishing this project together with the lead. Each session
is one teammate doing one task well, then handing off cleanly so the next one can start
fresh. Here's how we work.

* **One task, done by you.** A session launched with « Read
  milestones/m<N>/m<N>_implementation_plan.md and execute M<N>-<id> » does that task itself —
  no sub-agents, no orchestrating, no waiting on background work. The one exception: the exact
  launch words « you are an orchestrator ».
* **Start from your block.** First act, before any other read: one grep of your heading —
  `grep -n "^## M<N>-<id> " milestones/m<N>/m<N>_implementation_plan.md` — for its type, rating
  and switch segment; where it has « · switch », apply the switch right then (PLAYBOOK §B.3).
  Then `python3 tools/pb/plan.py show M<N>-<id>` prints your start read: the plan's header
  without its Flow table, then your block (a v12 trial, PLAYBOOK §16). Then your `Read:` line. Open a neighbouring block only when yours points at it. Repo facts exist so
  you never explore the tree.
* **Your task is one thread.** If it turns out to be two — a context switch, a second kind of
  artifact, a shared constant whose consumers you don't own — that's a sizing miss, not extra
  work for you. Finish the first thread, name the second as the split in your handoff, stop.
* **Your type says what you may write** (PLAYBOOK §0; the loop flags the rest). BUILD: what your
  `Deliver:` names, never the check that grades you; BLOCKED is a legitimate end. CHECK: your
  report, D blocks and moves, never a fix. PLAN: blocks, contract, Rules, your report, never
  code. MOVE: text moved byte for byte, a pointer where it stood, never reworded.
* **Test the way the team tests.** One harness, one routine (PLAYBOOK §4 Rule 4): iterate on
  your scope alone until it's green, red-arm it (`verify.py --redarm <scope>`), make your plan
  edits, then run `verify.py --changed` once — the scopes your changes touch; that run is your
  claim about the tree (the complete loop runs at phase-closing V and TR blocks). Rated below
  `(usual)`, a red claim ends your try: trail into your `Carried flags:`, rating `(usual)`, back
  to TODO (PLAYBOOK §0). A late fix is re-verified on the scopes it could have moved, each in its
  own block. Add a scope, never a second runner. Don't re-run a suite already green on the same tree.
* **Change the plan through the plan tool.** `tools/pb/plan.py` — extracted from PLAYBOOK annex
  §A by your project's `TH` — sets a status, closes a block (status, handoff and register in one
  call), files, moves or stubs a block and lints the plan, the Flow row kept in sync. Its calls
  are in the plan's Repo facts, and a wrong call prints the right one — never read its code or
  its help (a call or flag the tools lack: your adoption has not run; use the plan's own routine,
  say so). Until it exists, edit the plan with your file-edit tool; an ad-hoc script over it is a defect.
* **Run what's yours to run.** Without a grant: the project's deterministic suites, GPU tests,
  one boot smoke (the project's `launch.py smoke` — probes the port first; never reuse or stop a
  stack you didn't start), the read-only probes the plan names, read-only git. Open
  `milestones/m<N>/logs/<TASK-ID>.log` before the command runs so a crash still leaves a
  trace; report ≤3 lines plus the path. A run over 10 minutes is the lead's, in one visible
  terminal (PLAYBOOK §4 Rule 4); so are installs, git writes, dev servers as acceptance, paid
  APIs, windowed launches, destructive ops and real data — or they need an OPT-C grant, and even
  then anything paid, long, windowed or irreversible is asked each time. Restore files with your
  file-edit tools, never `git checkout` / `git restore`.
* **Say what you ran, and where.** Every command block carries its tag on the line above the
  fence: `[ALREADY RUN — PASS|FAIL (<what>) on <box>]` · `[NOT RUN — for you]` ·
  `[NOT RUN — owed on <other box>]`. The fence holds the command and nothing else, so it
  pastes and runs. Re-measure the tag when you hand over — a tag written when the block was
  authored is a plan, not a report. A skipped suite is `NOT RUN (<missing dependency>)`, never
  green; failed-then-passed reports both; ran nothing → say so. Every verdict names the
  machine it was measured on.
* **Hand back a verdict, not a dump.** Runners print one counts line, failures and skips only
  when non-zero, and end with `=== GO ===` or `=== NO-GO: <reason> ===` (exit 0 / non-zero),
  derived from the counts: zero passes is NO-GO; fewer cases than `Pass:` expects is NO-GO.
  `--json` is for agents, never the default. Criteria live on their own `Pass:` / `Fail:`
  lines, with a refusal arm where refusing is a legitimate outcome. Evidence from synthetic
  fixtures or a source read alone is `NOT PROVEN (synthetic | source only)`, not PASS.
* **The contract is our shared agreement.** `m<N>_contrat.md` fixes every schema, endpoint,
  formula and string. Wrong or ambiguous → ask. A frozen passage your milestone's own goal
  requires changing is declared, not escalated (PLAYBOOK §9): one override line in the current
  contract's §0, a mirror line in Superseded and in your handoff, then carry on. Still ask for
  unsettled product forks, changes outside the milestone's scope, and anything touching
  persisted data or migrations. Where the plan header reads `Changes by asking: every block`, you
  may also propose a small, durable change to the contract or the plan by asking — never a
  bypass that makes a failing thing pass. A guard or check you declare isn't done until you've
  shown it red under an injected bug (`verify.py --redarm`), in the same task.
* **Ask well, and don't stall.** A genuine fork → the question tool (none: numbered options in
  your message), up to four questions per call, one decision each, options (a)/(b) with your
  recommendation first, in plain words (PLAYBOOK §12); if the turn must end, `Status: IN
  PROGRESS (awaiting: <question>)`. A cheap-to-reverse default → take it, declare it in the
  handoff, keep going. A fact the disk holds (shell, version, path) is probed, not asked. Never
  make the lead re-key data the system already has. A request to author a task is not a request
  to build it.
* **A stated direction is a premise.** Raise a real risk once, in ≤3 lines, then execute; a
  restated instruction is final. Quote the lead's words; label a paraphrase as your reading.
  Superseded surface is retired `N/A — superseded by <decision, date>` with a traceable
  citation, never by editing a read-only file.
* **Found a bug outside your scope? File it.** A measured, reproducible defect becomes a `D` block you write —
  symptom, repro, suspected cause, the files you opened, its `Caused by:` (the block whose work it repairs, else
  `unknown`), and in its heading, right after its type tag (`**BUILD**`), its rung of the header's `Models:`
  line by PLAYBOOK §0's rubric — `(usual)` while the cause is not measured, else rule (3)'s (`usual rung` before
  a ladder) — then ` · switch` where the plan's headings carry one — sized like any task and placed where it
  will run (usually right after your block; elsewhere under an ordering note). You write the spec; you don't
  build it. Something measured and not filed goes in your handoff with the reason.
* **Handoff.** ≤8 lines inline: what exists now, deviations, every finding you measured and
  didn't act on (with why), what the next teammate needs, the model and level you ran on,
  `Next:`. Detail → `milestones/m<N>/tasks/<TASK-ID>.md`. Same edit: `plan.py close` (status,
  handoff, the Pipeline state's open-D register), then `plan.py flag` for any flag aimed at a
  later task, into its `Carried flags:`. Edit only your own `Status:`/`Handoff:` plus what the
  playbook lets you append (a `D` you file, a `TC<n>` the header budget fires); a CHECK block
  (V, TA, TL, the TW scribe) may move blocks to keep the plan runnable top to bottom — move only.
* **Statuses:** TODO · IN PROGRESS · DONE · BLOCKED · DEFERRED (wake: <condition>) · N/A —
  `plan.py status` stamps the date and time, and the lint refuses any other word. `- Status:` is
  the first line of every block; one blank line separates blocks.
* **Dense and structured, not short.** No line cap on `.md`; density and structure do the
  work — closed phases as stubs, detail in `tasks/<ID>.md`, shared warnings once in Rules, the
  Pipeline state a register that never repeats a handoff. Code keeps its ceilings (≤6 files /
  ≤600 new lines per task). Findings → `reports/`, raw output → `logs/`, images → `images/`.
  Never copy `models/`, `.venv/`, `data/` — the project's `scratch_copy.sh` (annex §A) does the
  rsync-exclude and symlink; name the scratch path in your handoff.
* **No agent memory.** If your agent keeps a memory of its own, don't write to it — the plan is the memory.
```

---

## §0 Block types, models and changes by asking

**A block's type says what it may write; its heading recommends a model and a level; the lead
picks.** Every block carries one of four types, set by its id (the table below), plus a LEAD tag
when the lead must act, and a rating — the rung of the plan's ladder its heading recommends
(below). The plan header carries two lines, set at bootstrap or by TZ and confirmed at TP:

```
Models: <agent> — <model>, <level> (gate) · <model>, <level> (usual) · <model>, <level> · … — strongest first; read <YYYY-MM-DD> from <its model pages | the probe> and the lead's answer
Changes by asking: PLAN only | every block — <one line why>
```

| Type | May write | May not | Block types (§3.1 ids) |
|---|---|---|---|
| BUILD | the files its `Deliver:` names — code, a tool, a report, research, an evidence note · the in-place contract amendment its `Deliver:` names and a §9 override line (the contract's §0, its Superseded mirror) · a D block · its log and task file · BLOCKED, when the task cannot be done as written | the check that grades it (its scope's cases, manifest or tests) unless `Deliver:` names them · any other change to what the plan or the contract says · what another block owns | T (an edit job is a T with `- Sizing: E`) · R · D · DBG · TH · TH-adopt · TR · TV · TD · a `TE` suffix (not `TE-V<N>`) |
| CHECK | its report or journal, its log and task file, its D blocks, block moves (E8), the re-arm of a correction block (§3.3); it runs anything the plan names and decides whether a result meets its bar | a fix of any kind · a verdict on work its own session changed · a softened bar | V · TA · TL · run-and-report gates · the TW scribe |
| PLAN | blocks — created, split, moved, re-scoped · the contract, Rules and the rules annex · forks put to the lead · its report (an interview's record, a red-team's findings), log and task file | product code or a tool (a BUILD block writes those) · a product fork the lead has not ruled | TI · TP · TC (the contract) · TG · TB · TJ · TZ · TE-V<N> · TB-V<N> · TM<n> |
| MOVE | text moved or stubbed through `plan.py`, which refuses a changed byte · by hand, byte for byte, every moved line diffed and counted (§11): a line hoisted to Rules, a Rules entry moved to `m<N>_rules.md` (its index line kept), the pointer a de-duplication leaves where the duplicate stood · the names a rename sweep replaces · its own Status, Handoff, log and task file | new text anywhere else · a reworded line | `TC<n>` compaction · archive moves · a `TE-V<N>`'s rename sweep |
| + LEAD go · walk · answers | a tag after the type: **go** — the lead approves before the block's high-impact act (a push, a paid or long run, a live plan's edit) · **walk** — the lead drives the app, a CHECK scribe journals · **answers** — the block closes on the lead's answers | acting before the go · closing before the answers | TW = `LEAD walk + CHECK` · Q = `LEAD answers` · TI, TJ = `PLAN + LEAD answers` · a bump's real run = `BUILD + LEAD go` |

- **The type follows the id.** `plan.py append` writes it from this table, `plan.py lint` flags a
  hand-typed type that disagrees, and the loop flags a changed path outside the block's type.
  Plans written before v12 keep `BUILDER`, `JUDGE` and `HUMAN` in their tags, read as aliases:
  the id sets the type and an old word never does — a JUDGE-tagged T is BUILD, a BUILDER-tagged
  TZ is PLAN, a JUDGE-tagged V is CHECK; HUMAN reads as a LEAD tag beside the id's type — and
  the tools never rewrite an existing tag.
- **A pre-v12 id keeps the kind its plan gave it** where v12's letters say otherwise or nothing —
  a `TV` a plan made its verification gate stays CHECK, never v12's UI/UX pass; a `TF` "final
  verification" is CHECK; ids are never renumbered (§3.1). The plan's `TE-V<N>` records each
  such id in one Repo facts line, `Kind kept: <id> = <TYPE> (<the kind its plan gave it>)`, and
  writes that type into the block's tag, the old word kept (`**CHECK · JUDGE**`); `plan.py lint`
  accepts a type that disagrees with the id only where such a line names the id.
- **The heading recommends; the lead picks.** The model is never set by the type. The lead
  launches each session on the model and level they pick — the heading's rating or any other — or
  names them for an orchestrator's batch, where "each heading's rating" is such a naming (§7);
  every handoff names the model and level it ran on. A rating never routes and never holds a
  block back: no block waits for a rung. **One exception, the switch:** in Claude Code a heading
  whose rating is followed by ` · switch` applies that rating itself — the switch, installed once
  per machine (§B.3), runs every request of the task session on the rung's model and level, and
  the task agent reads them back with `rung_record.py now` — and the lead's pick becomes the
  override: the lead deletes ` · switch` from a heading to run that block on the picker's model
  and level, the rating kept. Never under-power a judgment or honesty gate, and
  never step a block up on a guess (below); your own block's type and rating are not yours to
  change, but for escalation's step up (below).
- **The ladder** is the header's `Models:` line — the rungs the lead runs, in the agent's own
  words: the agent (a §B.3 row), then each rung `<model>, <level>` strongest first (`Sonnet 5.5,
  medium`; a model that takes no level, `<model>, none`; an agent's Auto mode, `Auto, <its
  tier>`, its handoff naming the model the agent reports), `(gate)` after the rung the lead runs
  judgment and honesty gates on and `(usual)` after the one they run the rest on (`(gate,
  usual)` where one rung is both), then the date and the source — the agent's model pages or the
  probe (§B.3) and the lead's answer, never memory: line-ups and prices move within weeks. The
  bootstrap writes it (§B.2 step 2); every adoption and every rating pass re-reads it on the day
  — a model retired, renamed or added, a price or a level changed → the line rewritten and
  dated, the lead's answer first where a rung they run changes. One rung up is the rung listed
  before; the top rung has none.
- **The rating — the rubric, first match wins:**
  (1) **escalation** (below) — a block whose claim run failed on a rung below `(usual)` →
  `(usual)`; one escalated after two failed attempts on `(usual)` or above → one rung above the
  rung it failed on;
  (2) **gates** — every CHECK and PLAN block, and every act that cannot be undone (a `LEAD go`: a
  push, a paid or long run, a live plan's edit) → `(gate)`;
  (3) **the cheapest rung that fits** — a BUILD or MOVE block → `Sizing: E` and a MOVE the
  ladder's lowest rung, any other BUILD block the highest rung below `(usual)` (`Sonnet 5.5,
  medium` and `Sonnet 5.5, high` under an `Opus 5.5, high (usual)`), unless rule (4) moved its
  class or a reason from this closed list holds, named by whoever rates it: a FROZEN passage, a
  schema, a persisted format or a migration changed · a D whose cause is not measured · a design
  the spec leaves open — a new module's interface, an algorithm with no reference; never a fixed
  spec's wording · secrets, money or data loss when the block's own failure can leak, spend or
  move, or destroy them — never the project's subject · numeric or precision semantics, or
  concurrency as threads, async or shared state — never a timing choice · a client's or a
  library's internals read to find a mechanism;
  (4) **the record** — a block with no reason from (3)'s list, of a class the record moved → one
  rung above (3)'s rung per move, `(usual)` at most. A class moves when, over ≥10 of its blocks
  on a rung below `(usual)`, its cost per DONE block — its sessions, an escalation's included,
  plus the D blocks traced to it — is above the same class's on `(usual)`, or above the plan's
  `(usual)` BUILD blocks' where the class has fewer than 10 there; the cost read by
  `tools/pb/rung_record.py` (annex §A) from the agent's session files where they are readable,
  else by list prices — a move when its first-try rate on the claim run is below that
  comparison's times the price ratio, the cheaper rung's list price per output token over the
  usual rung's, read that day (1 between two levels of one model);
  (5) **the rest** — a block with a reason from (3)'s list → `(usual)`.
  A class is a type and `E` or not, read per rung — the model *and* the level; a ladder with no
  rung below `(usual)` keeps rule (3)'s blocks on it. The record is the phase-closing V's table
  (§2.3).
- **No step up on a guess.** A block rates above the cheapest rung that fits only by rules
  (1)–(4), each named by its rater in its handoff or task file — rule (3) with its reason from
  the list; rule (2) names itself, by the block's type. A check, an adversarial pass or a rating
  pass that finds a cheaper rating risky with none of them to name records an observation with
  its reason — never a defect, never a re-rating up. "No record yet" is never a reason: the
  record starts on the cheaper rung, and rules (1) and (4) price a miss there. Size is §2.1's: a
  block that looks too big for its rung is split, never stepped up.
- **Who rates — the filer, in the edit that files the block.** The bootstrap, every block it
  writes; TZ, the next plan's — in full for an S plan, which has no TB (§2.5), by type alone
  (rule (2), and rule (3) with its list left to TB) for an M or L plan; TG by type alone, leaving
  the list and the record to TB, the pass after it in another session, which rates every block
  at the seed and at every re-run (§2.1);
  then every block filed later, by its filer — a D first, a V, a `TC<n>`, a `TR<n>`, a split's
  halves, a bump's chain (§3.2, §5). A block that would otherwise rate more than ~15 blocks in
  its own session — an authoring or an adoption — files a `TM<n>` to run next instead.
- **The rating pass, `TM<n>` (PLAN, template §14.3),** re-reads the ladder and rewrites the
  rating of every open block from the rubric — its heading, `Sizing:`, `Deliver:`, `Verify:` and
  `Carried flags:`, and the record — and nothing else; the register's `Model ratings:
  <YYYY-MM-DD> by <id>` line names the last block that rated the plan (the bootstrap, TB, a
  `TE-V<N>`, a `TM<n>`). Filed by any agent on: ≥5 carried flags added to open BUILD blocks since
  that line — a flag whose `[<from>, <date>]` stamp, bold or with a backticked id, is later than
  the line's date (one stamped the pass's own day was there to read; an unstamped flag counts
  only while the register has no such line — a pass read it, after that it is history); a flag
  on a CHECK, PLAN or MOVE block or a `LEAD go` never counts — rule (2) pins the gate's rating,
  and a MOVE's class is its type alone, which no flag moves —
  tunable in Rules as `Rating refresh: <n> flags` · a ladder change (a model added or gone,
  another agent) · a block over the ~15 above · a phase-closing V whose record moves a class
  (rule (4)), which files it · the lead's word. It runs next, after a pending
  `TC<n>`; `plan.py lint` stays red at the flag count until one is TODO or IN PROGRESS (§11).
- **Escalation carries the trail.** Below `(usual)`, the block's first NO-GO claim run — §4 Rule
  4's `verify.py --changed`, or its `Verify:` list where no scope declares paths; never a scoped
  run while it works — ends the try on that rung, with no second attempt there: the session writes
  its trail into the block's own `Carried flags:`, rates it `(usual)`, sets it back to TODO and
  stops; the next session starts fresh. On `(usual)` and above, two failed fix attempts on the
  same bug in one session → the next attempt runs in a fresh session with both attempts' full
  trail in its `Carried flags:`, the escalating session rating the block one rung up — never a
  third same-session retry, never a restart from nothing. Either way the lead picks the next
  session's model, and an escalation buys a stronger rung, never wider writes.
- **Changes by asking.** `PLAN only` (default): PLAN blocks propose changes to the contract or the
  plan. `every block`: every block may propose a small, durable change to the contract or the
  plan **by asking**, then apply exactly what the lead approved (§9). What it never buys: a
  bypass — weakening a check, relaxing a `Pass:` band, deleting a case, narrowing a `Deliver:`,
  renaming a clause so it stops biting. Persisted data and migrations still ask; `Deliver:`
  exactly still holds; sizing still applies. The plan states the line and one line of reason. A
  plan written before v12 reads its `Role mode:` line so: `ALL-JUDGE` is `every block`; `MIXED`,
  or no such line, is `PLAN only`.
- **Edit jobs (`E`).** A BUILD block whose spec names the exact files and functions and the
  shape of the diff · ≤2 files, ≤150 new lines · no new module, dependency, schema or string ·
  verified by an existing scope. TG marks it `- Sizing: E` as the block's second line; rule (3)
  rates it on the ladder's lowest rung.
- **Sizing differs by type** (§2.1): BUILD blocks split by independent failure classes and
  artifact kinds; PLAN and CHECK blocks split on "two subjects, two consumers", never on line count.
- **Cost never overrides bite.** A token or model pass may split a block further, never merge or
  keep whole a block that fails §2.1's tests.
- **The rating is a trial** (v12.1, §16) — kept at the v13 bump only if its keep test holds: over
  ≥20 blocks of a class on a cheaper rung, their cost per DONE block at or below what rule (4)
  compares it with, and D blocks per task no higher. v12's two model trials are folded into the
  rubric: the cheaper model where the work allows is rule (3), its price is rule (4)'s record, and
  size is §2.1's (no step up on a guess, above).

---

## §1 Repository layout

```
AGENTS.md                          # the instruction file: §C, generated by the bootstrap and at every bump
CLAUDE.md · GEMINI.md              # the one line `@AGENTS.md` each — generated the same way
PLAYBOOK.md                        # this file, byte-identical to canonical
tools/pb/                          # the toolkit — extracted by TH from annex §A; the project's own to fix or extend, never copied between projects
milestones.md                      # master tracker (name recorded in the plan's Repo facts)
milestones/
  m<N>/
    m<N>_implementation_plan.md    # the execution driver (one per milestone)
    m<N>_rules.md                  # full text of this plan's rules, grants, derogations (§11) — the plan's Rules is the index
    m<N>_contrat.md                # the contract (when the size class needs one)
    m<N>_carryover.md              # information the lead injects mid-milestone (§2.3)
    plan_archive*.md               # closed blocks, moved verbatim (§11) — plan_archive.md is the index when parts exist
    reports/<topic>.md             # knowledge offload — one topic per file
    reports/walk<k>.md             # live-walk journal (§8, E10)
    reports/review<k>.md           # review-page remarks, verbatim (§8, TV)
    tasks/<TASK-ID>.md             # per-task detail — the normal home of a handoff's detail
    logs/<TASK-ID>.log             # full self-run output; logs/loop_times.jsonl is the runner's timing record
    images/<TASK-ID>-<name>.png    # captures — git-ignored; images/tv<k>/ holds a review page's set
docs/agent/                        # one ≤1-page doc per module + README.md index (+ testing.md, written by TH)
archive/                           # retired trackers, specs, playbooks — never deleted
```

- **`logs/`, `reports/`, `tasks/` and `images/` exist from the first task of every milestone** —
  created by the bootstrap or the TZ that authors the plan; TP verifies. A milestone that closes
  with no log and no report has not exercised §4 Rule 4; that is a TP or V finding.
- **The tracker** is navigation + the lead's worklist (§4 Rule 10) + short dated deltas after any
  prompt that changes code, decisions or state. A destructive act outside the pipeline (a dataset
  deleted, a service wiped) gets its one-line delta the moment it happens. Every gate opens by
  checking the tracker's milestone row against the plan's Pipeline state and fixes it in the same
  edit; a PARKED / DEFERRED / close-out line is written from the plan's `Status:` fields, never
  from memory. History compresses into per-milestone files; knowledge lives in `reports/`.
- **`docs/agent/` pages**: Purpose · Interface (grep-verified names) · State · Invariants and
  seams · Gotchas — written for a teammate with zero context. Read the page before the module.
- **Measurement outputs and corpora never mix**: inputs are frozen fixtures; raw run outputs go
  to an untracked `output/` path. Run and output folder names use the lead's local time, in the
  zone the plan's Repo facts name; instants inside artifacts are UTC-Z. A naming change lands only
  after the current run-set completes; a record is identified by a content-derived id, never by
  folder sort order.
- **Where project law lives — the plan, and only the plan.** Facts (tracker name, shells,
  machines, product language, paths, spelling deviations) → `## Repo facts`. Hazards, dated and
  root-caused → `## Repo facts › Hazards`. Grants, derogations, standing rules → `## Rules`
  (index) + `m<N>_rules.md` (text). TZ carries all three forward (§2.4); TP checks the carry.
  Nothing local is ever edited into the instruction files or `PLAYBOOK.md` (§15).
- **Parallel product tracks** → OPT-E. **Sibling research tracks** → OPT-F. **Paid client or
  resale** → OPT-G.

---
## §2 Milestone lifecycle

### §2.1 Opening gates — in order, before any other task
For a plan built from scratch: **TI → TP → seed lot (architecture · contract · TH · TG) → TB →
execution.** TB acts on the pipeline TG writes, so it runs after TG. A conditional **TI · lead
interview** (PLAN + LEAD answers, §2.2) comes first whenever a plan is authored from scratch, so
TP red-teams an informed plan. Every gate also re-checks the plan's standing conditional
triggers (TL, cost ceilings, pending re-runs, module activations), the tracker row (§1) and
every step only the lead can do that is still open — put again as a live question (§12). Gates
change nothing on their own authority: each proposed change goes to the lead by the question
tool (§12), up to four per call, and only what is approved is applied.

1. **TP · plan red-team (PLAN).** Attack the plan: contradictions, missing tasks, loops, false
   assumptions — every claim is wrong until it survives. Opens by re-verifying every Repo-facts
   line its findings rely on. Flags on sight: a `Verify:` that cannot fail (§8) · a `Deliver:`
   item no check names (§3.2) · a deferred item already reconfirmed once (§2.6 P8 — cite, don't
   re-argue) · gates outnumbering builds (TP prints the ratio; above 1:1 the plan says why in one
   line or loses gates) · a phase that adds or changes screens with no `TV` UI/UX pass, or more
   than one live `TW` (§8) · a second file at the repo root that calls itself law or playbook
   (§B) · missing `logs/ reports/ tasks/ images/` · a stale tracker row · a previous plan's fact,
   hazard or rule that was neither carried nor retired with a reason (§2.4) · a header past its
   budget and margin whose `TC<n>` is not the next block to run (§11). A TP question that freezes a clause restates
   its consequence in one sentence.
2. **TB · task-size & token pass (PLAN).**
   Every BUILD block must be a bite a builder gets right the first time. The numbers are
   ceilings: ≈≤6 files / ≤600 new lines including tests; a cross-cutting seam is priced by call
   sites touched and splits by consumer group above ~8. The operative tests, all of which hold:
   (i) **one artifact kind** in `Deliver:` — a module, a schema, a screen, a doc page;
   (ii) **≤2 independent failure classes**;
   (iii) **no context switch** inside the session — "finish X, now switch to Y" is a split;
   (iv) **the ruling-dependency test decides where to cut** — if the second half needs an
   *artifact* from the first, split there; if it needs a *ruling*, don't. Split by dependency or
   operation, never by theme;
   (v) **the `Deliver:` file list is grep-verified**, never estimated;
   (vi) **then one notch smaller** — can one deliverable leave for its own block without creating
   a ruling dependency? Then it does. The concrete target is the edit job (§0 `E`): TB counts how
   many of the plan's blocks qualify.
   Named exceptions, declared on the block's own `Sizing exception:` line or the block splits:
   contract-forced indivisibility · a producer/consumer pair whose split creates an invisible
   defect · no green intermediate build state · a migration and its cross-tenant test in one
   session. An exception block is followed at once by a targeted V (CHECK). **D blocks
   are sized the same way** — a fix that is two operations is `D<n>a/b`, split by the spawner or by
   the next TB, each placed where it runs.
   **TB should find nothing.** TG sized to these tests and ran this table on its own output; a TB
   that finds ≥2 splits names the TG defect in its handoff, and the next TG carries it as a flag.
   TB re-fires when TG appends ≥3 blocks · a `Deliver:` moves between blocks (the depending
   block's `Verify:`/`Pass:` is re-derived in the same edit) · a `TE-V<N>` has run and the
   version changed the sizing rules — that re-run is the `TB-V<N>` (§3.1), filed by the TE. A
   pending re-run is a blocking gate written **as a block in the plan** by the task that
   triggered it, under itself, with a Flow row (E1, E8); one re-run discharges every firing that
   happened while it was pending.
   **The token half** (once, at the seed): hoist per-block boilerplate to Rules, de-duplicate the
   header, keep the binding **"considered and REJECTED (false economy)"** list (a later agent
   proposing an item on it is re-opening a closed decision), check the header against its budget
   (§11) before the first task runs. TB also audits type tags against the ids (§0), flags
   un-failable `Verify:` lines and any `Deliver:` item no check names (§3.2).
   **The rating** (at the seed and at every re-run): every block rated by §0's rubric — TG wrote
   each rung by type alone — and the register's `Model ratings:` line written.

### §2.2 Seed tasks (the first milestone, or any plan built from scratch)
**TI · lead interview (PLAN + LEAD answers, first)** — gather every on-disk fact first, then ask
for the real mechanics and reasoning, up to four questions per call, in plain words, never
feature wish-lists; answers land verbatim in `reports/<subject>_interview.md`, each behaviour
mapped MVP / later / dropped / open, open items routed onward (§2.6 P9). At bootstrap TI also
proposes the size class and the `Changes by asking:` line (§0). Fenced: it never amends a
contract, decides a fork or writes code. A pivot that only reshapes an ongoing milestone runs
the *dynamic* TI instead (E9).
Then: adversarial pass (PLAN, may rewrite downstream specs — documented, never product decisions)
→ architecture (PLAN) → contract (PLAN, §9) → **TH · test harness and toolkit (BUILD, §4 Rule 4,
annex §A: the tools extracted, the manifests written)** →
**TG · task generation (PLAN)**: writes the implementation pipeline sized to §2.1's tests from
line one — every `Deliver:` path opened, kinds and failure classes counted, the ruling-dependency
test applied, one notch smaller, `E` blocks marked, each heading's rung by type alone (§0 — TB
rates the rest), each `Deliver:` item mapped to the Verify step or `Pass:` line that checks it
(or `none — <why>`) — closes by running TB's table on its own output, in its handoff, and
inserts a V after each lot (E2) and a `TV` UI/UX pass in each phase
that adds or changes screens (§8). A lot is as long as the work decomposes into micro blocks; TG
errs toward more blocks, not fewer.
**The seed lot closes with a dependency gate:** the milestone's dependency table (name · license ·
cost · exit path) cleared in one question call (§12); installs from the approved table then skip
§4 Rule 2's per-package round-trips.

### §2.3 Execution
Pre-written blocks run in order (§6), interleaved with dynamic blocks per E1–E10 (§5). **Every
executed task has a block in the plan** — a lead-directed or ad-hoc task that ran without one gets
its block written retroactively, marked "as executed", before the next gate. The plan's **Pipeline
state** is a register of one-line pointers (§11): next task · counters · the open-D register
(every open D / BLOCKED item as id · one line · what unblocks it) · outstanding commit gates ·
carryover pointer · awaiting-lead · the last rating (`Model ratings:`, §0) — never a handoff, a
history or a ratio's prose; synced in the same edit as every handoff, so relaunching stalled
work never depends on anyone's memory.

**The complete loop runs at the phase's close.** Every phase-closing V runs `verify.py --all`
once and records, from `plan.py` and `verify.py report`, wall clock per task (the Status stamps)
and the complete loop's seconds on each box; then it runs `rung_record.py report` and keeps its
table in its report — per class and rung: blocks, first-try passes on the claim run (§4 Rule 4's
step 5), cost per DONE block, D blocks traced — the record §0's rubric rates from (rule (4)). A
complete loop over the budget stated in Rules → the V appends a `TR` (§4 Rule 4); a class the
record moves → a `TM<n>` (§0).

**The lead's eye lands early — after the agent's own look.** Each phase that adds or changes
screens ends with a **`TV` UI/UX pass (§8)**: the agent captures the screens, looks at every one
itself, files what does not fit and hands the lead a page of only what needs their eyes; the
**one live `TW` is the user gate** (§2.4). TP verifies both.

**The header stays within budget.** Above the first task block ≤600 lines, Pipeline state ≤60
(tunable in Rules). `plan.py lint` goes red when a budget is exceeded by more than the margin —
100 lines unless Rules tune it (§11) — and no `TC<n>` is TODO; the red is discharged by filing the
`TC<n>` (§11), which any agent may do — and that `TC<n>` runs next, before any other block, an
adoption chain's included.

Information the lead injects mid-milestone (an external review, new constraints) lands in
`m<N>_carryover.md` — §1 anti-relitigation list (settled, do not re-open) + §2 real carryover —
consumed by the closing TZ.

### §2.4 Close-out
Final V (CHECK, whole-milestone contract-vs-code) → **push (§10)** → **TW · the user gate (§8)**:
the lead runs the increment and approves; "tests pass" is never "done" → documentation task
(align tracker and docs with what shipped — doc edits pass the ask-gate, §4 Rule 6) → **TZ ·
next-plan authoring (PLAN)**: authors `m<N+1>_implementation_plan.md` fresh — Repo facts
re-measured on disk, never copied · every rule, grant and hazard of the closing plan carried by
citation into the new Rules index and `m<N+1>_rules.md`, or retired with a reason, or replaced by
"now PLAYBOOK §x" · no "(M<N>: …)" notes accreted onto inherited text · header under budget from
line one · blocks sized to §2.1 with `E` marked and rated (§0: an S plan in full, an M or L plan
by type alone, its TB rating the rest) · the ladder re-read on the day. TZ splits into `TZa`
(Rules, Repo facts, inheritance ledger) and `TZb` (the sized pipeline), both PLAN, only when one
TZ fails §2.1's size test. The next plan's own TP is its red-team. A milestone that forks ends
with one TZ per fork.
**Close clean:** the closing block marks every block not DONE as DONE or N/A with a pointer to
where its work landed, and names any acceptance walk never played — so no later reader mistakes
finished work for debt, or unwalked for walked. **Known-red at close:** a milestone may close
with a red gate only if the next plan's first lot owns each red item as a named block — an
inherited-debt register, one line per item, in both plans — and the tracker row reads
`DONE (debt: <n>)`.

### §2.5 Size classes — proportionality made mechanical
The bootstrap interview or TZ assigns each milestone a class; the lead confirms it at the gate.
- **X (exploratory)** — throwaway work below milestone law: one running `.md` log, "resume here"
  handoffs, one command at a time. No plans, gates, contracts or tracker. Promote to a real track
  the moment the work stops being throwaway; the two tiers never mix in one directory.
- **S (≤~4 tasks, ≤2 sessions)** — TP only (may open the first session); frozen-decisions block
  inline instead of a contract; no TB/TZ — so the bootstrap or the TZ that authors it rates every
  block (§0); journal style allowed.
- **M (≤~10 tasks)** — TP + TB; contract required; TZ optional.
- **L** — the full apparatus: TI → TP → seed lot → TB, contract, planned V cadence, final V, doc
  task, TZ. Dynamic rules (§5) and the user gate apply at every class except X.

**Splitting a milestone** on task or session count is legitimate only if each half keeps its own
judgeable user-facing gate. "Do not split" is a valid answer to a recount.

### §2.6 Direction changes — retire, don't defend
The failure this section prevents: the apparatus defending code the lead has already decided to
delete — baselines of the very surface a milestone replaces, comparators reverse-engineered to
have a "before", scaffolding multiplying faster than the surface it protects.
- **P1 · A stated direction is a premise.** Settled input, not a fork to arbitrate. Don't re-open
  it, re-litigate it, ask it again in new words, or build protective work around it. Name a real
  risk once, ≤3 lines, then execute; a restated instruction is final.
- **P2 · The only reasons to push back:** irreversible data loss · a legal or regulatory duty · a
  safety issue · a cost the lead demonstrably has not seen. Once, concretely, with the cheapest
  alternative that still does what was asked. "It breaks a convention" or "we'd lose the baseline"
  are not on the list.
- **P3 · Retire the superseded surface.** The superseding task marks it `N/A — superseded by
  <decision> (<date>)` in one edit: contract sections, baselines, corpora, fixtures, pending D/V
  that only covered it. Retiring is the default response to a pivot — never a Q, never BLOCKED.
  Retired ≠ deleted: the block stays with its reason. Two conditions or it is not a retirement:
  (i) `<decision>` cites the lead traceably (prompt and date, a Q answer, an interview line) —
  your inference is not a decision; (ii) a read-only file is never edited to retire it — the mark
  lands in the current contract's §0 and the plan's Superseded section.
- **P4 · No baseline of a corpse.** Capture a baseline or comparator only when the thing it
  measures will still exist at the end of the milestone and is not slated for replacement by a
  stated direction. If the surface is being replaced, the comparator is the new acceptance
  criterion.
- **P5 · Rewrites inherit no verification debt.** A declared rewrite starts from its own
  acceptance criteria; the superseded tests retire with the code they covered. A rewrite
  qualifies only if the lead declared it or the Superseded section already lists the old surface —
  an agent never awards itself the label mid-task. A refactor is not a rewrite.
- **P6 · Proportionality.** Ceremony scales to the risk of the change and how much surface
  survives it, never to the age of the code. Before adding a gate, baseline, corpus or V, say in
  one line what mistake it would catch and what that mistake costs; no answer → don't add it.
- **P7 · No clause manufactures the lead's labour.** A step that makes the lead hand-type or re-key
  data the system already holds is a defect — propose the automated path or drop the step.
- **P8 · Deferred stays deferred.** Once a gate reconfirmed a deferred item with no new evidence,
  later plans cite the ruling in one line.
- **P9 · Deferred ideas are captured, fenced and routed** — the words land verbatim · tiered
  (seam-only now / later / someday) with an explicit no-code fence · routed to a named future
  milestone · a research-only block when technically open · rationale in its own report.

The bookkeeping of a direction change is a **TJ · direction ruling (PLAN + LEAD answers, §3.1)**: the N/A
marks, the §9 override lines, the Superseded section, and the ruling's **negative scope** — what
it explicitly did not retire, so nobody widens it silently. P1–P9 loosen §4's engineering taste,
§8's stance, §9's freeze discipline and OPT-A's baselines by the lead's recorded decision; they
never touch honesty about results, the contract over live surface, or the user gate.

---

## §3 Task format

### §3.1 Ids
`M<N>-<id>`; the letters encode the kind, and the kind sets the type (§0): **T** implementation
(an `E` edit job is a T with `- Sizing: E`) · **R** web research · **D** dynamic debug (one precise
bug) · **DBG** a debug task the lead directed (§12) · **V** validation on a scope · **Q** question
for the lead (unattended or orchestrated runs only, §12) · **TI** lead interview · **TJ** direction
ruling · **TW** the live walk (the lead + a CHECK scribe — one per milestone, the user gate) ·
**TV** UI/UX pass (BUILD, §8) · **TD** show-off demo (BUILD, OPT-B) · **TH** test harness /
**TH-adopt** for an existing project · **TR** test-time pass · **TC** the contract · **TC<n>**
compaction · **TM<n>** rating pass (§0) · **TG** task generation · gates **TP / TA / TB / TZ**
(TA = research red-team of R outputs) · **TL** legal and data-basis clearance (OPT-D) ·
**TE-V<N>** playbook-version adoption · **TB-V<N>** the sizing re-run after an adoption when the
version changed the sizing rules — `<N>` the whole version, a point version's included
(`TE-V12.1`, §15).
Splits append letters (`T6a/T6b`); other out-of-sequence tooling gets a `TE` suffix (`M1-TE`,
`M1-TE2`, `M1-TE-<name>` — BUILD). Ids are minted by grep — the first free number, never guessed,
never renumbered (an `X` in an instruction is a placeholder for that grep) — and never reused; a
pre-v12 id keeps the kind its plan gave it (§0). In prose an id carries its milestone prefix
(`M1-D17`, never `D17`).

### §3.2 Task block
```markdown
## M<N>-<id> · <title> · **<TYPE>** · <model>, <level> · (FIRST | AFTER <id> | LAST | ∥ set-X)
- Status: TODO
- Sizing: E                       <edit job — omit otherwise>
- Ask (verbatim): <the lead's exact words when the task executes a request | omit when none>
- Carried flags: <flags aimed at this task — each acknowledged in the handoff | omit when none>
- Sizing exception: <one of §2.1's named exceptions + why | omit when none>
- Read: this file (rules + this task) + <exact files; a file over ~500 lines by section or line range — nothing else>
- Deliver: <exact artifacts, every path grep-verified; ONE artifact kind; each item → the Verify step that checks it, or `none — <why>`; nothing more>
- Verify: <numbered bash blocks, one command each, naming their harness scope; EACH followed by
  its own `Pass:` and `Fail:` lines — the expected count in `Pass:`, a refusal/N-A arm where a
  refusal is a legitimate outcome, BLOCKED where the task may prove impossible as written | "none">
- Adversarial: <the strongest reason this could be wrong, and the measurement that would catch it>
- Handoff: <placeholder — ≤8 lines inline when done; detail → tasks/<ID>.md>
```
`- Status:` is the first line under the heading; one blank line separates blocks; `Adversarial:`
is mandatory on implementation blocks in M/L milestones. A LEAD tag after the type says what the
lead does — `BUILD + LEAD go`, `CHECK + LEAD go`, `LEAD walk + CHECK`, `PLAN + LEAD answers` (§0).
**The rating** follows the type tag: `<model>, <level>`, a rung of the header's ladder, the model
then the level and no other word (`Sonnet 5.5, medium`) — written by the block's filer from §0's
rubric, the cheapest rung that fits unless one of its rules (1)–(4) is named (§0, no step up on
a guess), a §14.3 template's slot filled the same way; a Q, which the lead answers, carries none.
**The switch** — ` · switch` right after the rating, before the order — is written where the
ladder's agent is Claude Code (§0, §B.3): the filer puts it after every rating it writes, a
§14.3 template's included (`… · **BUILD** · Sonnet 5.5, medium · switch · (AFTER M3-T7)`), and
the switch runs that block's whole session on the rating's model and level, nothing typed. The
lead deletes it to bypass the switch on that block, the rating kept — `plan.py lint` wants the
rating on every open block, never the switch. Nothing writes it back: a rating pass, an adoption
or a tool that rewrites a rating keeps the segment as it finds it, there or gone; only a block's
filer, in the edit that files it, writes a new one.
A plan with no ladder yet — a bump's chain lands before the plan's adoption — carries the rung's
role in its place, `gate rung`, `usual rung` or `lowest rung`, and its adoption converts each; a
bump's chain is rated by type: its `TE-V<N>` the gate rung, its `TC<n>` — a MOVE — the lowest
rung (§0's rule (3)), its `TH-adopt` blocks the usual rung — a placeholder, since no role word
names the rung below it, which the adoption, run before them, re-rates by the rubric.

### §3.3 Statuses
`TODO → IN PROGRESS (YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM) → DONE (YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM, started HH:MM)` — `plan.py
status` writes both stamps, and `stub` keeps a block's own date before any `archived by` clause ·
`IN PROGRESS (awaiting: <question>)` · `BLOCKED` (+ what unblocks it) · `BLOCKED-D<n>` ·
`DEFERRED (wake: <condition>)` — stopped on purpose with a named trigger, for tasks, milestones
and tracks · `N/A` (cancelled, one-line reason). A stall is written `BLOCKED` or `IN PROGRESS
(awaiting: …)`, never in prose or another language: `plan.py lint` refuses a status outside this
list on a block still to run, and reads a finished block (DONE, N/A — bold, notes, the plan's own
language) and an old plan's prose or phase Flow as history, never a failure. A verifier may flip a
conditional correction block from N/A-candidate to TODO and rewrite its `Deliver:`.

---
## §4 How every agent works (Rules 0–10)

0. **You are the task agent.** Fixed launch prompt (§14.5). Execute your one task; don't spawn,
   delegate or orchestrate, and never end a turn waiting for background work you will not hear
   from (§6). "Fresh independent agents" describes how the lead launches sessions — one per task.
   The one exception: the launch words « you are an orchestrator » (§7).
1. **Start from your block.** First one grep of your own heading, then the switch where the
   heading has one (§B.3), then `plan.py show <ID>`, which prints your start read — the plan's
   header without its Flow table (the Flow is the gates' jump index), then your block — in place of the
   header end to end (a v12 trial, §16: kept only if lines read per task fall over the next 20
   tasks with no D, question or BLOCKED traced to a fact the view left out). Then your `Read:`
   line (+ files your task owns). Open a rule's full text in `m<N>_rules.md` at the numbers your
   block touches. A neighbouring block only when yours points at it. Read the `docs/agent/` page
   for a module, not the module; open code only for the slice you edit. An audit task may read
   every file in a directory its `Read:` line names. The one sanctioned end-to-end read is `Read:
   this file (whole)`, which only gates and TE-V<N> carry.
2. **Scope.** Produce exactly the `Deliver:` items — no extra files, dependencies, refactors or
   improvements. Your task is one thread: a block that turns out to be two is a sizing miss —
   finish the first, name the second as the split, stop. A task that changes a shared constant
   names every downstream consumer it can affect or carries a flag to their owner. No new
   dependency without approval — normally granted in bulk by the seed dependency gate; outside
   the table, ask with license, cost, free tier and exit path (default OSS, commercial use
   permitted; flag copyleft). Every code task ships its tests in the same task, as scopes of the
   one harness.
3. **The contract is our shared agreement** (§9). Implement as written; wrong or ambiguous → ask.
   Prior-milestone contracts are read-only.
4. **Run what's yours, once, and say what you ran.**
   **Bounded self-run without a grant:** the project's deterministic suites (no live server, no
   network, no real data) · one boot smoke — `tools/pb/launch.py smoke`: probe the port first,
   start, wait for an honest ready answer, stop; never reuse or stop a stack you didn't start (a
   reused stack carries the lead's real `.env`) · the read-only probes the plan names · read-only
   git (§10). Open `logs/<TASK-ID>.log` before the command runs. Report ≤3 lines plus the path,
   and still hand over the numbered blocks for the lead's authoritative run — a block that needs
   project infra up gets its stack-up block first. Cheap before expensive: a headless smoke before
   a windowed capture, a parse check before a run that costs the lead a window. Not yours without
   an OPT-C grant: installs, git writes, dev servers as acceptance, e2e against a server you
   booted as acceptance, destructive ops, paid APIs, a run over 10 minutes (the long-run rule
   below), windowed launches, non-dev data. Restore a file with your file-edit tools, never
   `git checkout` / `git restore`.
   **The harness — extracted once by `TH` from annex §A, extended by everyone.** One runner
   (`tools/pb/verify.py` over the project's manifest): scopes as manifest entries, `<scope>` /
   `--changed` / `--all` / `--case` / `--redarm`, count-derived verdict, jobs derived from the box,
   exclusivity declared per scope, the log path opened first, wall clock per scope recorded with
   the box and interpreter it ran on. A **coverage anchor** records what each scope last checked:
   `--changed` runs every scope a changed path touches, a path no scope has checked is NO-GO, and
   every baseline is a named commit, never HEAD. Each scope declares its planted bugs; `--redarm`
   runs them in a scratch copy and each must end NO-GO. The loop flags a changed test, case or
   manifest entry the block's `Deliver:` does not name, and a changed path outside the block's
   type (§0); each `FLAG` line is answered in the handoff — the `Deliver:` item the write served,
   or the D filed. Every task adds a scope or a case; nobody writes a second runner or a one-off
   probe that duplicates a scope. `docs/agent/testing.md` says how to add a scope and what each
   costs.
   **The routine — the same order for every task:**
   1. author the artifact and its scope, with the scope's planted bugs;
   2. iterate on **your scope alone** until it is green — never a wider run while the artifact
      moves;
   3. red-arm the scope while you iterate: `verify.py --redarm <scope>` — the clean copy GO, each
      plant NO-GO; when a declaration was wrong, re-run the campaign, not the loop. This is the
      iteration run; the proof is step 5's;
   4. make every structural plan edit (`plan.py`: status, blocks you file, register, flags);
   5. **run `verify.py --changed` once, last** (+ the smoke when the stack changed) — every scope
      your changed paths touch, and the plants of every scope the block added or changed: the
      proof. This run is your claim about the tree you hand over. The complete loop (`--all`)
      runs at phase-closing V and TR blocks, never per task;
   6. write the handoff prose and numbers from that run;
   7. a fix that lands after step 5 is re-verified **scoped**, one block per scope that could have
      moved, each with its own tag and verdict. The loop is not a re-run tool; a second green
      loop on an unchanged tree is named in the handoff as waste. The order is the whole of the
      cost — coverage is untouched: no `Pass:` weakened, no case dropped, no scope optional.
   **`TR` · test-time pass (BUILD).** Fires when the complete loop exceeds the budget line in
   Rules on its box, or on the lead's word — no other trigger. Measures where the loop spends its
   wall clock (per scope, per span, cold and warm), names the causes, **files the fixes as
   separate blocks**, re-measures with the same
   harness, restates the budget. Standing levers, priced at their measured gain: walk each tree or
   resolve each path once per process · run independent arms concurrently, report in declared
   order · a case-level filter for a RED arm · one seeded stack per run · settle windows measured,
   never guessed. Not a saving: dropping a case, softening a `Pass:`, a global settle cut, shared
   state that makes verdicts order-dependent. Every declined lever is recorded with its number so
   it is not re-argued.
   **Run report — mandatory.** Every handed-over block carries its tag on the prose line
   immediately above the fence; the fence holds the command and nothing else. Tags:
   `[ALREADY RUN — PASS|FAIL (<what>) on <box>]` · `[NOT RUN — for you]` · `[NOT RUN — owed on
   <other box>]`. The tag is re-measured at handover, never copied from the authored spec. N
   near-identical runs report per run; a skipped suite reports `NOT RUN (<missing dependency>)`;
   failed-then-passed reports both; ran nothing → "nothing run". Every verdict names its box — a
   green runner is a claim about an environment, not a tree.
   **Verdict, not a dump — mandatory.** Every runner, harness, probe or verify command you write,
   port or hand over ends in `=== GO ===` or `=== NO-GO: <reason> ===` (exit 0 / non-zero), after
   one counts line and one line per failure and per skip, only when non-zero. The verdict is
   derived from the measured counts — a fixed verdict string is a defect; zero passes is NO-GO;
   fewer cases than `Pass:` expects is NO-GO. `--json` behind a flag, never the default. A command
   that cannot reduce to a verdict (`git status`, a build log) says in one line what good looks
   like. Three tiers: **PASS** against the real artifact on real-shaped data · **NOT PROVEN
   (synthetic)** on fixtures alone · **NOT PROVEN (source only)** for a code-read of a compiled or
   packaged build. Criteria live on their own `Pass:` / `Fail:` lines with a refusal arm where a
   refusal is legitimate. Any self-run beyond a few seconds shows live stderr progress —
   display-only, never inside a measured span.
   **Long runs — one rule, never dictated again per project.** GPU tests and smoke tests are
   yours to run. A run expected to take over 10 minutes — a training, a long CPU job — is the
   lead's: hand it over as one command for one visible terminal, with a fine progress bar that
   still shows there when the output is piped through `tee`; never start it in the background or
   in a second terminal. Such a run shares the GPU when its memory fits and never waits on
   another job's load, and it saves its full state at a bounded interval so it resumes from
   where it stopped.
5. **Probe the platform before phrasing commands.** Confirm the lead's shell and machine by one
   read-only probe (`ls`, `uname`, `$PSVersionTable`), never by asking. Never default to POSIX.
   PowerShell: no bare `&&`, `.\` prefix; no subshell — `Push-Location …; try { … } finally {
   Pop-Location }`; POSIX: `(cd … && …)`. Unknown → both variants, one per block. Handed git blocks
   are plain `git add` / `git commit` / `git push`, never `git -C <path>`. After a machine switch,
   confirm build artifacts postdate the fix before re-running.
6. **Ask well — and clear the bar first.** A question must clear three bars: the plan and the
   contract genuinely leave it open · the answer conditions your deliverable · being wrong is
   expensive or hard to reverse. Then the question tool (§12), up to four questions per call, one
   decision each, options + your recommendation; if the turn must end, `IN PROGRESS (awaiting:)`.
   Below the bar → the sane default, declared in the handoff (`Assumed: <X>, because <Y> —
   reversible by <Z>`). Never re-ask an answered question in new words (§2.6 P1). Money, pricing,
   client-facing and legal/safety calls always clear the bar. **Ask before deleting or editing
   documents**: no deletion, and no edit to contracts, README or build docs without the lead's
   confirmation — itemized; the tracker is yours to keep current (§1). Exempt: an `N/A` mark
   under §2.6 P3, a declared override under §9, a banner the lead pre-authorized you to remove
   once its condition is met. Before writing a new script, search the repo for an existing one —
   and the toolkit first.
